<div id="p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown mw-portlet mw-portlet-lang"  >

					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Founding Father, 5th president of the United States</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For other people named James Monroe, see <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="James Monroe (disambiguation)">James Monroe (disambiguation)</a>.</div>

</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Rank</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Major_(United_States)" title="Major (United States)">Major</a> (Army)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Colonel_(United_States)" title="Colonel (United States)">Colonel</a> (Militia)</li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Battles/wars</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1116488514">.mw-parser-output .treeview ul{padding:0;margin:0}.mw-parser-output .treeview li{padding:0;margin:0;list-style-type:none;list-style-image:none}.mw-parser-output .treeview li li{background:url("https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f2/Treeview-grey-line.png")no-repeat 0 -2981px;padding-left:21px;text-indent:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .treeview li li:last-child{background-position:0 -5971px}.mw-parser-output .treeview li.emptyline>ul>.mw-empty-elt:first-child+.emptyline,.mw-parser-output .treeview li.emptyline>ul>li:first-child{background-position:0 9px}</style><div class="treeview">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">American Revolutionary War</a>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Trenton" title="Battle of Trenton">Battle of Trenton</a>&#160;(<a href="/wiki/Wounded_in_action" title="Wounded in action"><abbr title="Wounded in action">WIA</abbr></a>)</li></ul></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>James Monroe</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/oʊ/: &#39;o&#39; in &#39;code&#39;">oʊ</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling">mən-<span style="font-size:90%">ROH</span></i></a>; April 28, 1758&#160;&#8211;&#32;July 4, 1831) was an American statesman, lawyer, diplomat, and <a href="/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States" title="Founding Fathers of the United States">Founding Father</a> who served as the fifth <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a> from 1817 to 1825, a member of the <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a>. He was the last president who was a Founding Father as well as the last president of the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_dynasty" title="Virginia dynasty">Virginia dynasty</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Strauss%E2%80%93Howe_generational_theory#Republican_Generation" title="Strauss–Howe generational theory">Republican Generation</a>; <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_James_Monroe" title="Presidency of James Monroe">his presidency</a> coincided with the <a href="/wiki/Era_of_Good_Feelings" title="Era of Good Feelings">Era of Good Feelings</a>, concluding the <a href="/wiki/First_Party_System" title="First Party System">First Party System</a> era of American politics. He is best known for issuing the <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a>, a policy of limiting European colonialism in the Americas. Previously he served as governor of <a href="/wiki/Virginia" title="Virginia">Virginia</a>, a member of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">United States Senate</a>, U.S. ambassador to France and Britain, the seventh secretary of state, and the eighth secretary of war.
</p><p>Monroe served in the <a href="/wiki/Continental_Army" title="Continental Army">Continental Army</a> during the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">American Revolutionary War</a>. He studied law under <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a> from 1780 to 1783 and served as a <a href="/wiki/List_of_delegates_to_the_Continental_Congress" title="List of delegates to the Continental Congress">delegate</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Continental_Congress" title="Continental Congress">Continental Congress</a> as a delegate to the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Ratifying_Convention" title="Virginia Ratifying Convention">Virginia Ratifying Convention</a>. He opposed the ratification of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Constitution">United States Constitution</a>. In 1790, he won election to the Senate where he became a leader of the Democratic-Republican Party. He left the Senate in 1794 to serve as President <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a>'s ambassador to France but was recalled by Washington in 1796. Monroe won the election as <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_Virginia" title="Governor of Virginia">Governor of Virginia</a> in 1799 and strongly supported Jefferson's candidacy in the <a href="/wiki/1800_United_States_presidential_election" title="1800 United States presidential election">1800 presidential election</a>.
</p><p>As President Jefferson's special envoy, Monroe helped negotiate the <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a>, through which the United States nearly doubled in size. Monroe fell out with his longtime friend <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">James Madison</a> after Madison rejected the <a href="/wiki/Monroe%E2%80%93Pinkney_Treaty" title="Monroe–Pinkney Treaty">Monroe–Pinkney Treaty</a> that Monroe negotiated with Britain. He unsuccessfully challenged Madison for the Democratic-Republican nomination in the <a href="/wiki/1808_United_States_presidential_election" title="1808 United States presidential election">1808 presidential election</a>, but he joined Madison's administration as Secretary of State in 1811. During the later stages of the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>, Monroe simultaneously served as Madison's Secretary of State and Secretary of War. Monroe's wartime leadership established him as Madison's heir apparent, and he easily defeated <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Party" title="Federalist Party">Federalist</a> candidate <a href="/wiki/Rufus_King" title="Rufus King">Rufus King</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1816_United_States_presidential_election" title="1816 United States presidential election">1816 presidential election</a>.
</p><p>During Monroe's tenure as president, the Federalist Party collapsed as a national political force and Monroe was re-elected, virtually unopposed, in <a href="/wiki/1820_United_States_presidential_election" title="1820 United States presidential election">1820</a>. As president, Monroe signed the <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Compromise" title="Missouri Compromise">Missouri Compromise</a>, which admitted Missouri as a slave state and banned slavery from territories north of the 36°30′ parallel. In foreign affairs, Monroe and Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a> favored a policy of conciliation with Britain and a policy of expansionism against the Spanish Empire. In the 1819 <a href="/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty" title="Adams–Onís Treaty">Adams–Onís Treaty</a> with Spain, the United States secured Florida and established its western border with <a href="/wiki/New_Spain" title="New Spain">New Spain</a>. In 1823, Monroe announced the United States' opposition to any European intervention in the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_American_wars_of_independence" title="Spanish American wars of independence">recently independent countries</a> of the Americas with the Monroe Doctrine, which became a landmark in American foreign policy. Monroe was a member of the <a href="/wiki/American_Colonization_Society" title="American Colonization Society">American Colonization Society</a> which supported the colonization of Africa by freed slaves, and <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a>'s capital of <a href="/wiki/Monrovia" title="Monrovia">Monrovia</a> is named in his honor.
</p><p>Following his retirement in 1825, Monroe was plagued by financial difficulties and died on July 4, 1831, in New York City—sharing a distinction with Presidents <a href="/wiki/John_Adams" title="John Adams">John Adams</a> and Thomas Jefferson of dying on the <a href="/wiki/Independence_Day_(United_States)" title="Independence Day (United States)">anniversary of U.S independence</a>. Historians have <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">generally ranked</a> him as an above-average president.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span></h2>
<p>James Monroe was born April 28, 1758, in his parents' house in a wooded area of <a href="/wiki/Westmoreland_County,_Virginia" title="Westmoreland County, Virginia">Westmoreland County</a>, <a href="/wiki/Virginia" title="Virginia">Virginia</a>. The marked site is one mile (1.6 km) from the unincorporated community known today as <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Hall,_Virginia" title="Monroe Hall, Virginia">Monroe Hall, Virginia</a>. The <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe_Family_Home_Site" title="James Monroe Family Home Site">James Monroe Family Home Site</a> was listed on the <a href="/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places" title="National Register of Historic Places">National Register of Historic Places</a> in 1979. His father Spence Monroe (1727–1774) was a moderately prosperous planter and slave owner who also practiced carpentry. His mother Elizabeth Jones (1730–1772) married Spence Monroe in 1752 and they had five children: Elizabeth, James, Spence, Andrew, and Joseph Jones.<sup id="cite_ref-Unger_2009_9–10_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Unger_2009_9–10-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Harry_Ammon_1971_p._577_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harry_Ammon_1971_p._577-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Birthplace_of_Monroe_historical_marker.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Birthplace_of_Monroe_historical_marker.jpg/220px-Birthplace_of_Monroe_historical_marker.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Birthplace_of_Monroe_historical_marker.jpg/330px-Birthplace_of_Monroe_historical_marker.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Birthplace_of_Monroe_historical_marker.jpg/440px-Birthplace_of_Monroe_historical_marker.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6016" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption>Marker designating the site of James Monroe's birthplace in <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Hall,_Virginia" title="Monroe Hall, Virginia">Monroe Hall, Virginia</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>His paternal great-great-grandfather Patrick Andrew Monroe emigrated to America from <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a> in the mid-17th century, and was part of an ancient Scottish clan known as <a href="/wiki/Clan_Munro" title="Clan Munro">Clan Munro</a>. In 1650 he patented a large tract of land in Washington Parish, <a href="/wiki/Westmoreland_County,_Virginia" title="Westmoreland County, Virginia">Westmoreland County, Virginia</a>. Monroe's mother was the daughter of James Jones, who immigrated from <a href="/wiki/Wales" title="Wales">Wales</a> and settled in nearby <a href="/wiki/King_George_County,_Virginia" title="King George County, Virginia">King George County, Virginia</a>. Jones was a wealthy architect.<sup id="cite_ref-Unger_2009_9–10_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Unger_2009_9–10-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Also among James Monroe's ancestors were <a href="/wiki/French_Huguenot" class="mw-redirect" title="French Huguenot">French Huguenot</a> immigrants, who came to Virginia in 1700.<sup id="cite_ref-Harry_Ammon_1971_p._577_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harry_Ammon_1971_p._577-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At age 11, Monroe was enrolled in Campbelltown Academy, the lone school in the county. He attended this school only 11 weeks a year, as his labor was needed on the farm. During this time, Monroe formed a lifelong friendship with an older classmate, <a href="/wiki/John_Marshall" title="John Marshall">John Marshall</a>. Monroe's mother died in 1772, and his father two years later. Though he inherited property, including slaves, from both of his parents, the 16-year-old Monroe was forced to withdraw from school to support his younger brothers. His childless maternal uncle, <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Jones_(Virginia)" class="mw-redirect" title="Joseph Jones (Virginia)">Joseph Jones</a>, became a surrogate father to Monroe and his siblings. A member of the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_House_of_Burgesses" class="mw-redirect" title="Virginia House of Burgesses">Virginia House of Burgesses</a>, Jones took Monroe to the capital of <a href="/wiki/Williamsburg,_Virginia" title="Williamsburg, Virginia">Williamsburg, Virginia</a>, and enrolled him in the <a href="/wiki/College_of_William_and_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="College of William and Mary">College of William and Mary</a>. Jones also introduced Monroe to important Virginians such as <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a>, <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Henry" title="Patrick Henry">Patrick Henry</a>, and <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a>. In 1774, opposition to the British government grew in the <a href="/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies" title="Thirteen Colonies">Thirteen Colonies</a> in reaction to the "<a href="/wiki/Intolerable_Acts" title="Intolerable Acts">Intolerable Acts</a>", and Virginia sent a delegation to the <a href="/wiki/First_Continental_Congress" title="First Continental Congress">First Continental Congress</a>. Monroe became involved in the opposition to <a href="/wiki/John_Murray,_4th_Earl_of_Dunmore" title="John Murray, 4th Earl of Dunmore">Lord Dunmore</a>, the colonial governor of Virginia, and took part in the storming of the <a href="/wiki/Governor%27s_Palace_(Williamsburg,_Virginia)" title="Governor&#39;s Palace (Williamsburg, Virginia)">Governor's Palace</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Revolutionary_War_service">Revolutionary War service</span></h3>
<p>In early 1776, about a year and a half after his enrollment, Monroe dropped out of college and joined the 3rd Virginia Regiment in the <a href="/wiki/Continental_Army" title="Continental Army">Continental Army</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> As the fledgling army valued literacy in its officers, Monroe was commissioned with the rank of lieutenant, serving under Captain <a href="/wiki/William_Washington" title="William Washington">William Washington</a>. After months of training, Monroe and 700 Virginia infantrymen were called north to serve in the <a href="/wiki/New_York_and_New_Jersey_campaign" title="New York and New Jersey campaign">New York and New Jersey campaign</a>. Shortly after the Virginians arrived, George Washington led the army in a retreat from <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a> into New Jersey and then across the <a href="/wiki/Delaware_River" title="Delaware River">Delaware River</a> into Pennsylvania. In late December, Monroe took part in a surprise attack on a <a href="/wiki/Hessian_(soldier)" title="Hessian (soldier)">Hessian encampment</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Trenton" title="Battle of Trenton">Battle of Trenton</a>. Though the attack was successful, Monroe suffered a severed artery in the battle and nearly died. In the aftermath, Washington cited Monroe and William Washington for their bravery, and promoted Monroe to captain. After his wounds healed, Monroe returned to Virginia to recruit his own company of soldiers.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> His participation in the battle was memorialized in <a href="/wiki/John_Trumbull" title="John Trumbull">John Trumbull</a>'s painting <i><a href="/wiki/The_Capture_of_the_Hessians_at_Trenton,_December_26,_1776" title="The Capture of the Hessians at Trenton, December 26, 1776">The Capture of the Hessians at Trenton, December 26, 1776</a></i> as well as <a href="/wiki/Emanuel_Leutze" title="Emanuel Leutze">Emanuel Leutze</a>'s 1851 <i><a href="/wiki/Washington_Crossing_the_Delaware_(1851_painting)" class="mw-redirect" title="Washington Crossing the Delaware (1851 painting)">Washington Crossing the Delaware</a>.</i><sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Capture_of_the_Hessians_at_Trenton_December_26_1776.jpeg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/The_Capture_of_the_Hessians_at_Trenton_December_26_1776.jpeg/300px-The_Capture_of_the_Hessians_at_Trenton_December_26_1776.jpeg" decoding="async" width="300" height="199" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/The_Capture_of_the_Hessians_at_Trenton_December_26_1776.jpeg/450px-The_Capture_of_the_Hessians_at_Trenton_December_26_1776.jpeg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/The_Capture_of_the_Hessians_at_Trenton_December_26_1776.jpeg/600px-The_Capture_of_the_Hessians_at_Trenton_December_26_1776.jpeg 2x" data-file-width="3000" data-file-height="1992" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/The_Capture_of_the_Hessians_at_Trenton,_December_26,_1776" title="The Capture of the Hessians at Trenton, December 26, 1776">The Capture of the Hessians at Trenton, December 26, 1776</a></i>, by <a href="/wiki/John_Trumbull" title="John Trumbull">John Trumbull</a>, showing Captain <a href="/wiki/William_Washington" title="William Washington">William Washington</a>, with a wounded hand, on the right and Lt. Monroe, severely wounded and helped by Dr. John Riker, left of center, behind the mortally wounded Hessian Colonel <a href="/wiki/Johann_Gottlieb_Rall" class="mw-redirect" title="Johann Gottlieb Rall">Johann Gottlieb Rall</a>. Rall is being helped by American Major <a href="/wiki/William_Stephens_Smith" title="William Stephens Smith">William Stephens Smith</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Lacking the wealth to induce soldiers to join his company, Monroe instead asked his uncle to return him to the front. Monroe was assigned to the staff of General <a href="/wiki/William_Alexander,_Lord_Stirling" title="William Alexander, Lord Stirling">William Alexander, Lord Stirling</a>. During this time he formed a close friendship with the <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_du_Motier,_Marquis_de_Lafayette" title="Gilbert du Motier, Marquis de Lafayette">Marquis de Lafayette</a>, a French volunteer who encouraged him to view the war as part of a wider struggle against religious and political tyranny. Monroe served in the <a href="/wiki/Philadelphia_campaign" title="Philadelphia campaign">Philadelphia campaign</a> and spent the winter of 1777–78 at the encampment of <a href="/wiki/Valley_Forge" title="Valley Forge">Valley Forge</a>, sharing a log hut with Marshall. After serving in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Monmouth" title="Battle of Monmouth">Battle of Monmouth</a>, the destitute Monroe resigned his commission in December 1778 and joined his uncle in Philadelphia. After the British <a href="/wiki/Capture_of_Savannah" title="Capture of Savannah">captured Savannah</a>, the Virginia legislature decided to raise four regiments, and Monroe returned to his native state, hoping to receive his own command. With letters of recommendation from Washington, Stirling, and <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton" title="Alexander Hamilton">Alexander Hamilton</a>, Monroe received a commission as a lieutenant colonel and was expected to lead one of the regiments, but recruitment again proved to be a problem. On Jones's advice, Monroe returned to Williamsburg to study law, becoming a protege of Virginia Governor Thomas Jefferson.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the British increasingly focusing their operations in the <a href="/wiki/Southern_United_States" title="Southern United States">Southern colonies</a>, the Virginians moved the capital to the more defensible city of <a href="/wiki/Richmond,_Virginia" title="Richmond, Virginia">Richmond</a>, and Monroe accompanied Jefferson to the new capital. As governor of Virginia, Jefferson held command over its militia, and made Monroe a colonel. Monroe established a messenger network to coordinate with the Continental Army and other state militias. Still unable to raise an army due to a lack of interested recruits, Monroe traveled to his home in King George County, and thus was not present for the British <a href="/wiki/Raid_of_Richmond" class="mw-redirect" title="Raid of Richmond">raid of Richmond</a>. As both the Continental Army and the Virginia militia had an abundance of officers, Monroe did not serve during the <a href="/wiki/Yorktown_campaign" title="Yorktown campaign">Yorktown campaign</a>, and, much to his frustration, did not take part in the <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Yorktown" title="Siege of Yorktown">Siege of Yorktown</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Although <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a> served as a courier in a militia unit at age 13, Monroe is regarded as the last U.S. president who was a <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">Revolutionary War</a> veteran, since he served as an officer of the Continental Army and took part in combat.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHart2005&#91;&#91;Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2021&#93;&#93;&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;&#91;&#91;Wikipedia:Citing_sources&#124;&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(August_2021)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;&#93;&#93;&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHart2005[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2021]]&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(August_2021)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;]]&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> As a result of his service, Monroe became a member of the <a href="/wiki/Society_of_the_Cincinnati" title="Society of the Cincinnati">Society of the Cincinnati</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Monroe resumed studying law under Jefferson and continued until 1783.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-log-cabin-76_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-log-cabin-76-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> He was not particularly interested in legal theory or practice, but chose to take it up because he thought it offered "the most immediate rewards" and could ease his path to wealth, social standing, and political influence.<sup id="cite_ref-log-cabin-76_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-log-cabin-76-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Monroe was admitted to the Virginia bar and practiced in <a href="/wiki/Fredericksburg,_Virginia" title="Fredericksburg, Virginia">Fredericksburg, Virginia</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriage_and_family">Marriage and family</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Elizabeth_Monroe.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Elizabeth_Monroe.jpg/220px-Elizabeth_Monroe.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Elizabeth_Monroe.jpg/330px-Elizabeth_Monroe.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Elizabeth_Monroe.jpg/440px-Elizabeth_Monroe.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1040" data-file-height="1300" /></a><figcaption>Elizabeth Kortright</figcaption></figure>
<p>On February 16, 1786, Monroe married <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_Monroe" title="Elizabeth Monroe">Elizabeth Kortright</a> (1768–1830) in New York City.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> She was the daughter of Hannah Aspinwall Kortright and Laurence Kortright, a wealthy trader and former British officer. Monroe met her while serving in the Continental Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After a brief honeymoon on <a href="/wiki/Long_Island,_New_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Long Island, New York">Long Island, New York</a>, the Monroes returned to New York City to live with her father until Congress adjourned. They then moved to Virginia, settling in <a href="/wiki/Charlottesville,_Virginia" title="Charlottesville, Virginia">Charlottesville, Virginia</a>, in 1789. They bought an estate in Charlottesville known as <a href="/wiki/Ash_Lawn%E2%80%93Highland" class="mw-redirect" title="Ash Lawn–Highland">Ash Lawn–Highland</a>, settling on the property in 1799. The Monroes had three children.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Eliza_Monroe_Hay" title="Eliza Monroe Hay">Eliza Monroe Hay</a> was born in Fredericksburg, Virginia, in 1786, and was educated in Paris at the school of <a href="/wiki/Madame_Campan" class="mw-redirect" title="Madame Campan">Madame Campan</a> during the time her father was the United States Ambassador to France. In 1808 she married <a href="/wiki/George_Hay_(Virginia_judge)" title="George Hay (Virginia judge)">George Hay</a>, a prominent Virginia attorney who had served as prosecutor in the trial of <a href="/wiki/Aaron_Burr" title="Aaron Burr">Aaron Burr</a> and later as a U.S. District Judge. She died in 1840.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>James Spence Monroe was born in 1799 and died sixteen months later in 1800.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Maria_Hester_Monroe_Gouverneur" title="Maria Hester Monroe Gouverneur">Maria Hester Monroe</a> (1802–1850) married her cousin <a href="/wiki/Samuel_L._Gouverneur" title="Samuel L. Gouverneur">Samuel L. Gouverneur</a> on March 8, 1820, in the White House, the first president's child to marry there.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Plantations_and_slavery">Plantations and slavery</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:OakHillfront.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/OakHillfront.jpg/220px-OakHillfront.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="148" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/9/92/OakHillfront.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="250" data-file-height="168" /></a><figcaption>Oak Hill Mansion</figcaption></figure>
<p>Monroe sold his small Virginia plantation in 1783 to enter law and politics. Although he owned multiple properties over the course of his lifetime, his plantations were never profitable. Although he owned much more land and many more slaves, and speculated in property, he was rarely on site to oversee the operations. Overseers treated the slaves harshly to force production, but the plantations barely broke even. Monroe incurred debts by his lavish and expensive lifestyle and often sold property (including slaves) to pay them off.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The labor of Monroe's many slaves were also used to support his daughter and son-in-law, along with a ne'er-do-well brother, Andrew, and his son, James.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the course of his presidency, Monroe remained convinced that slavery was wrong and supported private manumission, but at the same time he insisted that any attempt to promote emancipation would cause more problems. Monroe believed that slavery had become a permanent part of southern life, and that it could only be removed on providential terms. Like so many other Upper South slaveholders, Monroe believed that a central purpose of government was to ensure "domestic tranquility" for all. Like so many other Upper South planters, he also believed that the central purpose of government was to empower planters like himself. He feared for public safety in the United States during the era of violent revolution on two fronts. First, from potential class warfare of the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolution" title="French Revolution">French Revolution</a> in which those of the propertied classes were summarily purged in mob violence and then preemptive trials, and second, from possible racial warfare similar to that of the <a href="/wiki/Haitian_Revolution" title="Haitian Revolution">Haitian Revolution</a> in which blacks, whites, then mixed-race inhabitants were indiscriminately slaughtered as events there unfolded.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Virginia_politics">Virginia politics</span></h3>
<p>Monroe was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_House_of_Delegates" title="Virginia House of Delegates">Virginia House of Delegates</a> in 1782. After serving on Virginia's Executive Council,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHart200512–13_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHart200512–13-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> he was elected to the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_the_Confederation" title="Congress of the Confederation">Congress of the Confederation</a> in November 1783 and served in Annapolis until Congress convened in Trenton, New Jersey in June 1784. He had served a total of three years when he finally retired from that office by the rule of rotation.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> By that time, the government was meeting in the temporary capital of <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a>. In 1784, Monroe undertook an extensive trip through Western New York and Pennsylvania to inspect the conditions in the Northwest. The tour convinced him that the United States had to pressure Britain to abandon its posts in the region and assert control of the Northwest.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> While serving in Congress, Monroe became an advocate for western expansion, and played a key role in the writing and passage of the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_Ordinance" title="Northwest Ordinance">Northwest Ordinance</a>. The ordinance created the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_Territory" title="Northwest Territory">Northwest Territory</a>, providing for federal administration of the territories West of Pennsylvania and North of the <a href="/wiki/Ohio_River" title="Ohio River">Ohio River</a>. During this period, Jefferson continued to serve as a mentor to Monroe, and, at Jefferson's prompting, he befriended another prominent Virginian, <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">James Madison</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHart200513–16_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHart200513–16-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Monroe resigned from Congress in 1786 to focus on his legal career, and he became an attorney for the state. In 1787, Monroe won election to another term in the Virginia House of Delegates. Though he had become outspoken in his desire to reform the Articles, he was unable to attend the <a href="/wiki/Constitutional_Convention_(United_States)" title="Constitutional Convention (United States)">Philadelphia Convention</a> due to his work obligations.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> In 1788, Monroe became a delegate to the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Ratifying_Convention" title="Virginia Ratifying Convention">Virginia Ratifying Convention</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHart200516–17_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHart200516–17-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> In Virginia, the struggle over the ratification of the proposed Constitution involved more than a simple clash between federalists and <a href="/wiki/Anti-federalists" class="mw-redirect" title="Anti-federalists">anti-federalists</a>. Virginians held a full spectrum of opinions about the merits of the proposed change in national government. Washington and Madison were leading supporters; <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Henry" title="Patrick Henry">Patrick Henry</a> and <a href="/wiki/George_Mason" title="George Mason">George Mason</a> were leading opponents. Those who held the middle ground in the ideological struggle became the central figures. Led by Monroe and <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Pendleton" title="Edmund Pendleton">Edmund Pendleton</a>, these "federalists who are for amendments" criticized the absence of a <a href="/wiki/Bill_of_rights" title="Bill of rights">bill of rights</a> and worried about surrendering taxation powers to the central government.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> After Madison reversed himself and promised to pass a bill of rights, the Virginia convention ratified the constitution by a narrow vote, though Monroe himself voted against it. Virginia was the tenth state to ratify the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Constitution">Constitution</a>, and all thirteen states eventually ratified the document.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Senator">Senator</span></h3>
<p>Henry and other anti-federalists hoped to elect a Congress that would amend the Constitution to take away most of the powers it had been granted ("commit suicide on [its] own authority", as Madison put it). Henry recruited Monroe to run against Madison for a House seat in the <a href="/wiki/1st_United_States_Congress" title="1st United States Congress">First Congress</a>, and he had the Virginia legislature <a href="/wiki/Gerrymandering_in_the_United_States" title="Gerrymandering in the United States">draw</a> a <a href="/wiki/Congressional_district" title="Congressional district">congressional district</a> designed to elect Monroe. During the campaign, Madison and Monroe often traveled together, and the election did not destroy their friendship. In <a href="/wiki/1789_Virginia%27s_5th_congressional_district_election" title="1789 Virginia&#39;s 5th congressional district election">the election for Virginia's Fifth District</a>, Madison prevailed over Monroe, taking 1,308 votes compared to Monroe's 972 votes. Following his defeat, Monroe returned to his legal duties and developed his farm in Charlottesville. After the death of <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">Senator</a> <a href="/wiki/William_Grayson" title="William Grayson">William Grayson</a> in 1790, Virginia legislators elected Monroe to serve the remainder of Grayson's term.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_George_Washington" title="Presidency of George Washington">presidency of George Washington</a>, U.S. politics became increasingly polarized between the supporters of Secretary of State Jefferson and
the <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Party" title="Federalist Party">Federalists</a>, led by Secretary of the Treasury Alexander Hamilton. Monroe stood firmly with Jefferson in opposing Hamilton's strong central government and strong executive. The <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a> coalesced around Jefferson and Madison, and Monroe became one of the fledgling party's leaders in the Senate. He also helped organize opposition to <a href="/wiki/John_Adams" title="John Adams">John Adams</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1792_United_States_presidential_election" title="1792 United States presidential election">1792</a>
election, though Adams defeated <a href="/wiki/George_Clinton_(vice_president)" title="George Clinton (vice president)">George Clinton</a> to win re-election as vice president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham19963–5_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham19963–5-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> As the 1790s progressed, the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolutionary_Wars" title="French Revolutionary Wars">French Revolutionary Wars</a> came to dominate U.S. foreign policy, with British and French raids both threatening U.S. trade with Europe. Like most other Jeffersonians, Monroe supported the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolution" title="French Revolution">French Revolution</a>, but Hamilton's followers tended to sympathize more with Britain. In 1794, hoping to find a way to avoid war with both countries, Washington appointed Monroe as his <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_France" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to France">minister (ambassador) to France</a>. At the same time, he appointed the anglophile Federalist <a href="/wiki/John_Jay" title="John Jay">John Jay</a> as his <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to the United Kingdom">minister to Britain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_to_France">Minister to France</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:James_Monroe_(1758-1831).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/James_Monroe_%281758-1831%29.jpg/170px-James_Monroe_%281758-1831%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="190" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/James_Monroe_%281758-1831%29.jpg/255px-James_Monroe_%281758-1831%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/James_Monroe_%281758-1831%29.jpg/340px-James_Monroe_%281758-1831%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1076" data-file-height="1200" /></a><figcaption>The earliest preserved portrait of James Monroe as Minister Plenipotentiary to France in 1794</figcaption></figure>
<p>After arriving in France, Monroe addressed the <a href="/wiki/National_Convention" title="National Convention">National Convention</a>, receiving a standing ovation for his speech celebrating <a href="/wiki/Republicanism" title="Republicanism">republicanism</a>. He experienced several early diplomatic successes, including the protection of U.S. trade from French attacks. He also used his influence to win the release of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Paine" title="Thomas Paine">Thomas Paine</a> and <a href="/wiki/Adrienne_de_La_Fayette" title="Adrienne de La Fayette">Adrienne de La Fayette</a>, the wife of the Marquis de Lafayette.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Months after Monroe arrived in France, the U.S. and Great Britain concluded the <a href="/wiki/Jay_Treaty" title="Jay Treaty">Jay Treaty</a>, outraging both the French and Monroe—not fully informed about the treaty prior to its publication. Despite the undesirable effects of the Jay Treaty on Franco-American relations, Monroe won French support for U.S. navigational rights on the <a href="/wiki/Mississippi_River" title="Mississippi River">Mississippi River</a>—the mouth of which was controlled by <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>—and in 1795 the U.S. and Spain signed <a href="/wiki/Pinckney%27s_Treaty" title="Pinckney&#39;s Treaty">Pinckney's Treaty</a>. The treaty granted the U.S. limited rights to use the port of <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHart200529–34_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHart200529–34-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Washington decided Monroe was inefficient, disruptive, and failed to safeguard the national interest. He recalled Monroe in November 1796.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Returning to his home in Charlottesville, he resumed his dual careers as a farmer and lawyer.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Jefferson and Madison urged Monroe to run for Congress, but Monroe chose to focus on state politics instead.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham19966–7_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham19966–7-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/John_Skey_Eustace" title="John Skey Eustace">John Skey Eustace</a> was the author of several pamphlets,<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> some designed to embarrass James Monroe:<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In 1798 Monroe published <i>A View of the Conduct of the Executive, in the Foreign Affairs of the United States: Connected with the Mission to the French Republic, During the Years 1794, 5, and 6 </i>. It was a long defense of his term as Minister to France. He followed the advice of his friend Robert Livingston who cautioned him to "repress every harsh and acrimonious" comment about Washington. However, he did complain that too often the U.S. government had been too close to Britain, especially regarding the Jay Treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971165–167_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971165–167-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Washington made notes on this copy, writing, "The truth is, Mr. Monroe was cajoled, flattered, and made to believe strange things. In return he did, or was disposed to do, whatever was pleasing to that nation, reluctantly urging the rights of his own."<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Confrontations_and_strife_with_Alexander_Hamilton">Confrontations and strife with Alexander Hamilton</span></h3>
<p>In November 1792, <a href="/wiki/Hamilton%E2%80%93Reynolds_affair" title="Hamilton–Reynolds affair">James Reynolds</a> and Jacob Clingman were arrested for counterfeiting and speculating in Revolutionary War veterans' unpaid back wages. Then-Senator Monroe and congressmen <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Muhlenberg" title="Frederick Muhlenberg">Frederick Muhlenberg</a> and <a href="/wiki/Abraham_B._Venable" title="Abraham B. Venable">Abraham Venable</a> investigated the charges. They found that <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton" title="Alexander Hamilton">Alexander Hamilton</a> had been making payments to James Reynolds, and suspected Hamilton was involved in the crimes.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> They asked him about it, and Hamilton denied involvement in the financial crimes, but admitted that he'd made payments to Reynolds, and explained he'd had an affair with Reynolds' wife, <a href="/wiki/Maria_Reynolds" title="Maria Reynolds">Maria</a>. James Reynolds had found out and was blackmailing him. He offered letters to prove his story. The investigators immediately dropped the matter, and Monroe promised Hamilton he would keep the matter private.
</p><p>Jacob Clingman told Maria about the claim she'd had an affair with Hamilton, and she denied it, claiming the letters had been forged to help cover up the corruption. Clingman went to Monroe about this. Monroe added that interview to his notes, and sent the entire set to a friend, possibly <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a>, for safekeeping. Unfortunately, the secretary who was involved in managing the notes of the investigation made copies and gave them to scandal writer <a href="/wiki/James_Callender" class="mw-redirect" title="James Callender">James Callender</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Five years later, shortly after Monroe was recalled from France, Callender published accusations against Hamilton based on those notes. Hamilton and his wife thought this was retaliation on the part of Monroe for the recall, and confronted him via letter. In a subsequent meeting between the two of them, where Hamilton had suggested each bring a "second", Hamilton accused Monroe of lying, and challenged him to a duel. While such challenges were usually hot air, in this case Monroe replied "I am ready, get your pistols." Their seconds interceded, and an arrangement was made to give Hamilton documentation on what had occurred with the investigation.
</p><p>Hamilton was not satisfied with the subsequent explanations, and at the end of an exchange of letters the two were threatening duels, again. Monroe chose <a href="/wiki/Aaron_Burr" title="Aaron Burr">Aaron Burr</a> as his second. Burr worked as a negotiator between the two parties, believing they were both being "childish", and eventually helped settle matters.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Governor_of_Virginia_and_diplomat_.281799.E2.80.931802.2C_1811.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Governor_of_Virginia_and_diplomat_(1799–1802,_1811)">Governor of Virginia and diplomat (1799–1802, 1811)</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Governor_of_Virginia">Governor of Virginia</span></h3>
<p>On a party-line vote, the Virginia legislature elected Monroe as <a href="/wiki/Governor_of_Virginia" title="Governor of Virginia">Governor of Virginia</a> in 1799. He would serve as governor until 1802.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham19967_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham19967-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The constitution of Virginia endowed the governor with very few powers aside from commanding the militia when the Assembly called it into action. But Monroe used his stature to convince legislators to enhance state involvement in transportation and education and to increase training for the militia. Monroe also began to give <a href="/wiki/State_of_the_State_address" title="State of the State address">State of the Commonwealth addresses</a> to the legislature, in which he highlighted areas in which he believed the legislature should act. Monroe also led an effort to create the state's first <a href="/wiki/Penitentiary" class="mw-redirect" title="Penitentiary">penitentiary</a>, and imprisonment replaced other, often harsher, punishments. In 1800, Monroe called out the state militia to suppress <a href="/wiki/Gabriel%27s_Rebellion" title="Gabriel&#39;s Rebellion">Gabriel's Rebellion</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Slave_rebellion" title="Slave rebellion">slave rebellion</a> originating on a plantation six miles from the capital of Richmond. Gabriel and 27 other enslaved people who participated were all hanged for treason.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> As Governor, Monroe secretly worked with President Thomas Jefferson to secure a location where free and enslaved African Americans suspected of "conspiracy, insurgency, Treason, and rebellion" would be permanently banished.
</p><p>Monroe thought that foreign and Federalist elements had created the <a href="/wiki/Quasi_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Quasi War">Quasi War</a> of 1798–1800, and he strongly supported <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a>'s candidacy for president in <a href="/wiki/1800_United_States_presidential_election" title="1800 United States presidential election">1800</a>. Federalists were likewise suspicious of Monroe, some viewing him at best as a French dupe and at worst a traitor.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> With the power to appoint election officials in Virginia, Monroe exercised his influence to help Jefferson win Virginia's <a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">presidential electors</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham19967–8_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham19967–8-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> He also considered using the Virginia militia to force the outcome in favor of Jefferson.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Jefferson won the 1800 election, and he appointed Madison as his Secretary of State. As a member of Jefferson's party and the leader of the largest state in the country, Monroe emerged as one of Jefferson's two most likely successors, alongside Madison.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Louisiana_Purchase_and_Minister_to_Great_Britain">Louisiana Purchase and Minister to Great Britain</span></h3>
<p>Shortly after the end of Monroe's gubernatorial tenure, President Jefferson sent Monroe back to France to assist Ambassador <a href="/wiki/Robert_Livingston_(1746%E2%80%931813)" class="mw-redirect" title="Robert Livingston (1746–1813)">Robert R. Livingston</a> in negotiating the <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a>. In the 1800 <a href="/wiki/Third_Treaty_of_San_Ildefonso" title="Third Treaty of San Ildefonso">Treaty of San Ildefonso</a>, France had acquired the territory of <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_(New_Spain)" title="Louisiana (New Spain)">Louisiana</a> from Spain; at the time, many in the U.S. believed that France had also acquired <a href="/wiki/West_Florida" title="West Florida">West Florida</a> in the same treaty. The American delegation originally sought to acquire West Florida and the city of <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a>, which controlled the trade of the <a href="/wiki/Mississippi_River" title="Mississippi River">Mississippi River</a>. Determined to acquire New Orleans even if it meant war with France, Jefferson also authorized Monroe to form an alliance with the British if the French refused to sell the city.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Meeting with <a href="/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Barb%C3%A9-Marbois" title="François Barbé-Marbois">François Barbé-Marbois</a>, the French foreign minister, Monroe and Livingston agreed to purchase the entire territory of Louisiana for $15&#160;million; the purchase became known as the <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a>. In agreeing to the purchase, Monroe violated his instructions, which had only allowed $9&#160;million for the purchase of New Orleans and West Florida. The French did not acknowledge that West Florida remained in Spanish possession, and the United States would claim that France had sold West Florida to the United States for several years to come. Though he had not ordered the purchase of the entire territory, Jefferson strongly supported Monroe's actions, which ensured that the United States would continue to expand to the West. Overcoming doubts about whether the Constitution authorized the purchase of foreign territory, Jefferson won congressional approval for the Louisiana Purchase, and the acquisition doubled the size of the United States. Monroe would travel to Spain in 1805 to try to win the cession of West Florida, but, with the support of France, Spain refused to consider relinquishing the territory.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After the resignation of <a href="/wiki/Rufus_King" title="Rufus King">Rufus King</a>, Monroe was appointed as the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_Great_Britain" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Ambassador to Great Britain">ambassador to Great Britain</a> in 1803. The greatest issue of contention between the United States and Britain was that of the <a href="/wiki/Impressment" title="Impressment">impressment</a> of U.S. sailors. Many U.S. merchant ships employed British seamen who had deserted or dodged conscription, and the British frequently impressed sailors on U.S. ships in hopes of quelling their manpower issues. Many of the sailors they impressed had never been British subjects, and Monroe was tasked with persuading the British to stop their practice of impressment. Monroe found little success in this endeavor, partly due to Jefferson's alienation of the British minister to the United States, <a href="/wiki/Anthony_Merry" title="Anthony Merry">Anthony Merry</a>. Rejecting Jefferson's offer to serve as the first governor of <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Territory" title="Louisiana Territory">Louisiana Territory</a>, Monroe continued to serve as ambassador to Britain until 1807.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1806 he negotiated the <a href="/wiki/Monroe%E2%80%93Pinkney_Treaty" title="Monroe–Pinkney Treaty">Monroe–Pinkney Treaty</a> with Great Britain. It would have extended the Jay Treaty of 1794 which had expired after ten years. Jefferson had fought the Jay Treaty intensely in 1794–95 because he felt it would allow the British to subvert <a href="/wiki/Republicanism_in_the_United_States" title="Republicanism in the United States">American republicanism</a>. The treaty had produced ten years of peace and highly lucrative trade for American merchants, but Jefferson was still opposed. When Monroe and the British signed the new treaty in December 1806, Jefferson refused to submit it to the Senate for ratification. Although the treaty called for ten more years of trade between the United States and the British Empire and gave American merchants guarantees that would have been good for business, Jefferson was unhappy that it did not end the hated British practice of impressment, and refused to give up the potential weapon of commercial warfare against Britain. The president made no attempt to obtain another treaty, and as a result, the two nations drifted from peace toward the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Monroe was severely pained by the administration's repudiation of the treaty, and he fell out with Secretary of State James Madison.<sup id="cite_ref-leibiger_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leibiger-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1808_election_and_the_Quids">1808 election and the Quids</span></h3>
<p>On his return to Virginia in 1807, Monroe received a warm reception, and many urged him to run in the <a href="/wiki/1808_United_States_presidential_election" title="1808 United States presidential election">1808 presidential election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> After Jefferson refused to submit the Monroe-Pinkney Treaty, Monroe had come to believe that Jefferson had snubbed the treaty out of the desire to avoid elevating Monroe above Madison in 1808.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Out of deference to Jefferson, Monroe agreed to avoid actively campaigning for the presidency, but he did not rule out accepting a draft effort.<sup id="cite_ref-Unger_2009_200–201_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Unger_2009_200–201-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> The Democratic-Republican Party was increasingly factionalized, with "<a href="/wiki/Old_Republicans" class="mw-redirect" title="Old Republicans">Old Republicans</a>" or "Quids" denouncing the Jefferson administration for abandoning what they considered to be true republican principles. The Quids tried to enlist Monroe in their cause. The plan was to run Monroe for president in the 1808 election in cooperation with the <a href="/wiki/Federalist_Party" title="Federalist Party">Federalist Party</a>, which had a strong base in New England. <a href="/wiki/John_Randolph_of_Roanoke" title="John Randolph of Roanoke">John Randolph of Roanoke</a> led the Quid effort to stop Jefferson's choice of Madison. The regular Democratic-Republicans overcame the Quids in the nominating caucus, kept control of the party in Virginia, and protected Madison's base.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Monroe did not publicly criticize Jefferson or Madison during Madison's campaign against Federalist <a href="/wiki/Charles_Cotesworth_Pinckney" title="Charles Cotesworth Pinckney">Charles Cotesworth Pinckney</a>, but he refused to support Madison.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham199610–11_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham199610–11-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Madison defeated Pinckney by a large margin, carrying all but one state outside of New England. Monroe won 3,400 votes in Virginia, but received little support elsewhere.<sup id="cite_ref-Unger_2009_200–201_60-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Unger_2009_200–201-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> After the election Monroe quickly reconciled with Jefferson, but their friendship endured further strains when Jefferson did not promote Monroe's candidacy to Congress in 1809.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Monroe did not speak with Madison until 1810.<sup id="cite_ref-leibiger_57-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leibiger-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Returning to private life, he devoted his attentions to farming at his Charlottesville estate.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Secretary_of_State_and_Secretary_of_War_.281811.E2.80.931817.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State_and_Secretary_of_War_(1811–1817)">Secretary of State and Secretary of War (1811–1817)</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Madison_administration">Madison administration</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_James_Madison" title="Presidency of James Madison">Presidency of James Madison</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Vanderlyn_-_James_Monroe_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/John_Vanderlyn_-_James_Monroe_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/220px-John_Vanderlyn_-_James_Monroe_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="258" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/John_Vanderlyn_-_James_Monroe_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/330px-John_Vanderlyn_-_James_Monroe_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/John_Vanderlyn_-_James_Monroe_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg/440px-John_Vanderlyn_-_James_Monroe_-_Google_Art_Project.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3759" data-file-height="4401" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Secretary Monroe by <a href="/wiki/John_Vanderlyn" title="John Vanderlyn">John Vanderlyn</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1816</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Monroe returned to the Virginia House of Burgesses and was elected to another term as governor in 1811, but served only four months. In April 1811, Madison appointed Monroe to his <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_United_States" title="Cabinet of the United States">cabinet</a> as Secretary of State in hopes of shoring up the support of the more radical factions of the Democratic-Republicans.<sup id="cite_ref-leibiger_57-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leibiger-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Madison also hoped that Monroe, an experienced diplomat with whom he had once been close friends, would improve upon the performance of the previous Secretary of State, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Smith_(Cabinet_member)" title="Robert Smith (Cabinet member)">Robert Smith</a>. Madison assured Monroe that their differences regarding the Monroe-Pinkney Treaty had been a misunderstanding, and the two resumed their friendship.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> The Senate voted unanimously (30–0) to confirm him. On taking office, Monroe hoped to negotiate treaties with the British and French to end the attacks on American merchant ships. While the French agreed to reduce the attacks and release seized American ships, the British were less receptive to Monroe's demands.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Monroe had long worked for peace with the British, but he came to favor war with Britain, joining with "war hawks" such as Speaker of the House <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a>. With the support of Monroe and Clay, Madison asked Congress to declare war upon the British, and Congress complied on June 18, 1812, thus beginning the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The war went very badly, and the Madison administration quickly sought peace, but were rejected by the British.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> The U.S. Navy did experience several successes after Monroe convinced Madison to allow the Navy's ships to set sail rather than remaining in port for the duration of the war.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> After the resignation of Secretary of War <a href="/wiki/William_Eustis" title="William Eustis">William Eustis</a>, Madison asked Monroe to serve in dual roles as Secretary of State and Secretary of War, but opposition from the Senate limited Monroe to serving as acting Secretary of War until Brigadier General <a href="/wiki/John_Armstrong_Jr." title="John Armstrong Jr.">John Armstrong</a> won Senate confirmation.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Monroe and Armstrong clashed over war policy, and Armstrong blocked Monroe's hopes of being appointed to lead an invasion of <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham199612_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham199612-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> As the war dragged on, the British offered to begin negotiations in <a href="/wiki/Ghent" title="Ghent">Ghent</a>, and the United States sent a delegation led by <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a> to conduct negotiations. Monroe allowed Adams leeway in setting terms, so long as he ended the hostilities and preserved American neutrality.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When the British burned the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Capitol" title="United States Capitol">U.S. Capitol</a> and the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a> on August 24, 1814, Madison removed Armstrong as Secretary of War and turned to Monroe for help, appointing him Secretary of War on September 27.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHart200552–53_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHart200552–53-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Monroe resigned as Secretary of State on October 1, 1814, but no successor was ever appointed and thus from October 1814 to February 28, 1815, Monroe effectively held both Cabinet posts.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHart200553–54_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHart200553–54-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Now in command of the war effort, Monroe ordered General <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a> to defend against a likely attack on <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a> by the British, and he asked the governors of nearby states to send their militias to reinforce Jackson. He also called on Congress to draft an army of 100,000 men, increase compensation to soldiers, and establish a new <a href="/wiki/National_bank" title="National bank">national bank</a> to ensure adequate funding for the war effort.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Months after Monroe took office as Secretary of War, the war ended with the signing of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Ghent" title="Treaty of Ghent">Treaty of Ghent</a>. The treaty resulted in a return to the <a href="/wiki/Status_quo_ante_bellum" title="Status quo ante bellum">status quo ante bellum</a>, and many outstanding issues between the United States and Britain remained. But Americans celebrated the end of the war as a great victory, partly due to the news of the treaty reaching the United States shortly after Jackson's victory in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans" title="Battle of New Orleans">Battle of New Orleans</a>. With the end of the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a> in 1815, the British also ended the practice of impressment. After the war, Congress authorized the creation of a national bank in the form of the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1816">Election of 1816</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1816_United_States_presidential_election" title="1816 United States presidential election">1816 United States presidential election</a></div>
<p>Monroe decided to seek the presidency in the 1816 election, and his war-time leadership had established him as Madison's heir apparent. Monroe had strong support from many in the party, but his candidacy was challenged at the 1816 Democratic-Republican <a href="/wiki/Congressional_nominating_caucus" title="Congressional nominating caucus">congressional nominating caucus</a>. Secretary of the Treasury <a href="/wiki/William_H._Crawford" title="William H. Crawford">William H. Crawford</a> had the support of numerous Southern and Western Congressmen, while Governor <a href="/wiki/Daniel_D._Tompkins" title="Daniel D. Tompkins">Daniel D. Tompkins</a> was backed by several Congressmen from New York. Crawford appealed especially to many Democratic-Republicans who were wary of Madison and Monroe's support for the establishment of the Second Bank of the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham199615–16_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham199615–16-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Despite his substantial backing, Crawford decided to defer to Monroe on the belief that he could eventually run as Monroe's successor, and Monroe won his party's nomination. Tompkins won the party's vice presidential nomination. The moribund Federalists nominated Rufus King as their presidential nominee, but the party offered little opposition following the conclusion of a popular war that they had opposed. Monroe received 183 of the 217 <a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">electoral votes</a>, winning every state but Massachusetts, Connecticut, and Delaware.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Since he previously served as an officer of the <a href="/wiki/Continental_Army" title="Continental Army">Continental Army</a> during the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">Revolutionary War</a> and as a <a href="/wiki/List_of_delegates_to_the_Continental_Congress" title="List of delegates to the Continental Congress">delegate</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Continental_Congress" title="Continental Congress">Continental Congress</a>, he became the last president who was a <a href="/wiki/Founding_Fathers_of_the_United_States" title="Founding Fathers of the United States">Founding Father</a>.
</p>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281817.E2.80.931825.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1817–1825)">Presidency (1817–1825)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_James_Monroe" title="Presidency of James Monroe">Presidency of James Monroe</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_affairs">Domestic affairs</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Democratic-Republican_Party_dominance">Democratic-Republican Party dominance</span></h4>
<p>Monroe largely ignored old party lines in making federal appointments, which reduced political tensions and augmented the sense of "oneness" that pervaded the United States. He made two long national tours to build national trust. At Boston, a newspaper hailed his 1817 visit as the beginning of an "<a href="/wiki/Era_of_Good_Feelings" title="Era of Good Feelings">Era of Good Feelings</a>". Frequent stops on his tours included ceremonies of welcome and expressions of good-will. The Federalist Party continued to fade during his administration; it maintained its vitality and organizational integrity in Delaware and a few localities, but lacked influence in national politics. Lacking serious opposition, the Democratic-Republican Party's Congressional caucus stopped meeting, and for practical purposes the party stopped operating.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Administration_and_cabinet">Administration and cabinet</span></h4>
<p>Monroe appointed a geographically balanced cabinet, through which he led the executive branch.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham199628–29_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham199628–29-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> At Monroe's request, Crawford continued to serve as Treasury Secretary. Monroe also chose to retain <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Williams_Crowninshield" title="Benjamin Williams Crowninshield">Benjamin Crowninshield</a> of Massachusetts as Secretary of the Navy and <a href="/wiki/Richard_Rush" title="Richard Rush">Richard Rush</a> of Pennsylvania as Attorney General. Recognizing Northern discontent at the continuation of the Virginia dynasty, Monroe chose John Quincy Adams of Massachusetts as Secretary of State, making Adams the early favorite to eventually succeed Monroe. An experienced diplomat, Adams had abandoned the Federalist Party in 1807 in support of Thomas Jefferson's foreign policy, and Monroe hoped that the appointment would encourage the defection of more Federalists. After General Andrew Jackson declined appointment as Secretary of War, Monroe turned to South Carolina Congressman <a href="/wiki/John_C._Calhoun" title="John C. Calhoun">John C. Calhoun</a>, leaving the Cabinet without a prominent Westerner. In late 1817 Rush became the ambassador to Britain, and <a href="/wiki/William_Wirt_(Attorney_General)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Wirt (Attorney General)">William Wirt</a> succeeded him as Attorney General.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham199621–23_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham199621–23-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> With the exception of Crowninshield, the rest of Monroe's initial cabinet appointees remained in place for the remainder of his presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham1996118–119_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham1996118–119-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Missouri_Compromise">Missouri Compromise</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Compromise" title="Missouri Compromise">Missouri Compromise</a></div>
<p>In February 1819, a bill to enable the people of the <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Territory" title="Missouri Territory">Missouri Territory</a> to draft a constitution and form a government preliminary to admission into the Union came before the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">House of Representatives</a>. During these proceedings, Congressman <a href="/wiki/James_Tallmadge,_Jr." class="mw-redirect" title="James Tallmadge, Jr.">James Tallmadge, Jr.</a> of New York "tossed a bombshell into the Era of Good Feelings"<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> by offering the <a href="/wiki/Tallmadge_Amendment" title="Tallmadge Amendment">Tallmadge Amendment</a>, which prohibited the further introduction of slaves into Missouri and required that all future children of slave parents therein should be free at the age of twenty-five years. After three days of rancorous and sometimes bitter debate, the bill, with Tallmadge's amendments, passed. The measure then went to the Senate, which rejected both amendments.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> A House–Senate <a href="/wiki/Conference_committee" class="mw-redirect" title="Conference committee">conference committee</a> proved unable to resolve the disagreements on the bill, and so the entire measure failed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz2004380_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2004380-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The ensuing debates pitted the northern "restrictionists" (antislavery legislators who wished to bar slavery from the Louisiana territories and prohibit slavery's further expansion) against southern "anti-restrictionists" (proslavery legislators who rejected any interference by Congress inhibiting slavery expansion).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz2004380,_386_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2004380,_386-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the following session, the House passed a similar bill with an amendment, introduced on January 26, 1820, by <a href="/wiki/John_W._Taylor_(politician)" title="John W. Taylor (politician)">John W. Taylor</a> of <a href="/wiki/New_York_(state)" title="New York (state)">New York</a>, allowing Missouri into the union as a <a href="/wiki/Slave_state" class="mw-redirect" title="Slave state">slave state</a>. Initially, Monroe opposed any compromise that involved restrictions on slavery's expansion in federal territories. The question had been complicated by the admission in December of <a href="/wiki/Alabama" title="Alabama">Alabama</a>, a slave state, making the number of slave and free states equal. In addition, there was a bill in passage through the House (January 3, 1820) to admit <a href="/wiki/Maine" title="Maine">Maine</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Free_state_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Free state (United States)">free state</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Southern congressmen sought to force northerners to accept slavery in Missouri by connecting Maine and Missouri statehood. In this plan, endorsed by Monroe, Maine statehood would be held hostage to slavery in Missouri. In February 1820 the Senate passed a bill for the admission of Maine with an amendment enabling the people of Missouri to form a state constitution. Before the bill was returned to the House, a second amendment was adopted on the motion of <a href="/wiki/Jesse_B._Thomas" title="Jesse B. Thomas">Jesse B. Thomas</a> of <a href="/wiki/Illinois" title="Illinois">Illinois</a>, excluding slavery from the <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Territory" title="Louisiana Territory">Louisiana Territory</a> north of the <a href="/wiki/Parallel_36%C2%B030%E2%80%B2_north" title="Parallel 36°30′ north">parallel 36°30′ north</a> (the southern boundary of Missouri), except within the limits of the proposed state of Missouri. The House then approved the bill as amended by the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
The legislation passed, and became known as "the <a href="/wiki/Missouri_Compromise" title="Missouri Compromise">Missouri Compromise</a>". Though Monroe remained firmly opposed to any compromise that restricted slavery anywhere, he reluctantly signed the Compromise into law (March 6, 1820) only because he believed it was the least bad alternative for southern slaveholders. The Missouri Compromise temporarily settled the issue of slavery in the territories.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHammond2019_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHammond2019-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Internal_improvements">Internal improvements</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright">
<div class="thumbinner" style="width: 232px;">
