<div class="thumbimage" style="width: 230px; height: 270px; overflow: hidden;">
<div style="position: relative; top: -47px; left: -35px; width: 300px"><div class="noresize"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:MONROE,_James-President_(BEP_engraved_portrait).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Bureau of Engraving and Printing (BEP) engraved portrait of Monroe as President" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/MONROE%2C_James-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/300px-MONROE%2C_James-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="300" height="367" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/MONROE%2C_James-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/450px-MONROE%2C_James-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/MONROE%2C_James-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/600px-MONROE%2C_James-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="7912" data-file-height="9681" /></a></span></div></div>
</div>
<div class="thumbcaption">
<div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:MONROE,_James-President_(BEP_engraved_portrait).jpg" title="File:MONROE, James-President (BEP engraved portrait).jpg"> </a></div><a href="/wiki/Bureau_of_Engraving_and_Printing" title="Bureau of Engraving and Printing">Bureau of Engraving and Printing</a> (BEP) engraved portrait of Monroe as President
</div>
</div>
</div>
<p>As the United States continued to grow, many Americans advocated a system of <a href="/wiki/Internal_improvements" title="Internal improvements">internal improvements</a> to help the country develop. Federal assistance for such projects evolved slowly and haphazardly—the product of contentious congressional factions and an executive branch generally concerned with avoiding unconstitutional federal intrusions into state affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-ArmyCoE_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ArmyCoE-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> Monroe believed that the young nation needed an improved infrastructure, including a transportation network to grow and thrive economically, but did not think that the Constitution authorized Congress to build, maintain, and operate a national transportation system.<sup id="cite_ref-JM:DA_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JM:DA-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> Monroe repeatedly urged Congress to pass an amendment allowing Congress the power to finance internal improvements, but Congress never acted on his proposal, in part because many congressmen believed that the Constitution did in fact authorize the federal financing of internal improvements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham1996165–166_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham1996165–166-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In 1822, Congress passed a bill authorizing the collection of tolls on the <a href="/wiki/Cumberland_Road" class="mw-redirect" title="Cumberland Road">Cumberland Road</a>, with the tolls being used to finance repairs on the road. Adhering to stated position regarding internal improvements, Monroe vetoed the bill.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham1996165–166_92-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham1996165–166-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In an elaborate essay, Monroe set forth his constitutional views on the subject. Congress might appropriate money, he admitted, but it might not undertake the actual construction of national works nor assume jurisdiction over them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJohnson1915309–310_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson1915309–310-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1824, the Supreme Court ruled in <i><a href="/wiki/Gibbons_v._Ogden" title="Gibbons v. Ogden">Gibbons v. Ogden</a></i> that the Constitution's <a href="/wiki/Commerce_Clause" title="Commerce Clause">Commerce Clause</a> gave the federal government the authority to regulate interstate commerce. Shortly thereafter, Congress passed two important laws that, together, marked the beginning of the federal government's continuous involvement in civil works. The <a href="/wiki/General_Survey_Act" title="General Survey Act">General Survey Act</a> authorized the president to have surveys made of routes for roads and canals "of national importance, in a commercial or military point of view, or necessary for the transportation of public mail". The president assigned responsibility for the surveys to the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Army_Corps_of_Engineers" title="United States Army Corps of Engineers">Army Corps of Engineers</a>. The second act, passed a month later, appropriated $75,000 to improve navigation on the <a href="/wiki/Ohio_River" title="Ohio River">Ohio</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mississippi_river" class="mw-redirect" title="Mississippi river">Mississippi rivers</a> by removing sandbars, snags, and other obstacles. Subsequently, the act was amended to include other rivers such as the <a href="/wiki/Missouri_River" title="Missouri River">Missouri</a>. This work, too, was given to the Corps of Engineers—the only formally trained body of engineers in the new republic and, as part of the nation's small army, available to serve the wishes of Congress and the executive branch.<sup id="cite_ref-ArmyCoE_90-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ArmyCoE-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Panic_of_1819">Panic of 1819</span></h4>
<p>Two years into his presidency, Monroe faced an economic crisis known as the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1819" title="Panic of 1819">Panic of 1819</a>, the first major depression to hit the country since the ratification of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Constitution" class="mw-redirect" title="United States Constitution">Constitution</a> in 1788.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham199681_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham199681-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> The panic stemmed from declining imports and exports, and sagging agricultural prices<sup id="cite_ref-JM:DA_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JM:DA-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> as global markets readjusted to peacetime production and commerce in the aftermath of the War of 1812 and the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971462_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971462-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz2004208,_215_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2004208,_215-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> The severity of the economic downturn in the U.S. was compounded by excessive <a href="/wiki/Speculation" title="Speculation">speculation</a> in public lands,<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDangerfield196582,_84,_86_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDangerfield196582,_84,_86-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> fueled by the unrestrained issue of paper money from banks and business concerns.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz2004206_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2004206-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDangerfield196587_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDangerfield196587-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Monroe lacked the power to intervene directly in the economy, as banks were largely regulated by the states, and he could do little to stem the economic crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Before the onset of the Panic of 1819, some business leaders had called on Congress to increase tariff rates to address the negative balance of trade and help struggling industries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham199683–84_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham199683–84-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> As the panic spread, Monroe declined to call a special session of Congress to address the economy. When Congress finally reconvened in December 1819, Monroe requested an increase in the tariff but declined to recommend specific rates.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham199684–86_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham199684–86-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> Congress would not raise tariff rates until the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1824" title="Tariff of 1824">Tariff of 1824</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham1996167_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham1996167-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> The panic resulted in high unemployment and an increase in bankruptcies and foreclosures,<sup id="cite_ref-JM:DA_91-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JM:DA-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDangerfield196582,_84,_85_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDangerfield196582,_84,_85-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> and provoked popular resentment against banking and business enterprises.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDangerfield196589–90_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDangerfield196589–90-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs">Foreign affairs</span></h3>
<p>According to historian William Earl Weeks, "Monroe evolved a comprehensive strategy aimed at expanding the Union externally while solidifying it internally". He expanded trade and pacified relations with Great Britain while expanding the United States at the expense of the Spanish Empire, from which he obtained Florida and the recognition of a border across the continent. Faced with the breakdown of the expansionist consensus over the question of slavery, the president tried to provide both North and South with guarantees that future expansion would not tip the balance of power between slave and free states, a system that, Weeks remarks, did indeed allow the continuation of American expansion for the best of four decades.<sup id="cite_ref-ncha-vol1_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ncha-vol1-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Treaties_with_Britain_and_Russia">Treaties with Britain and Russia</span></h4>
<p>Monroe pursued warmer relations with Britain in the aftermath of the War of 1812.<sup id="cite_ref-sdmilestone_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sdmilestone-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> In 1817, the United States and Britain signed the <a href="/wiki/Rush%E2%80%93Bagot_Treaty" title="Rush–Bagot Treaty">Rush–Bagot Treaty</a>, which regulated naval armaments on the <a href="/wiki/Great_Lakes" title="Great Lakes">Great Lakes</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lake_Champlain" title="Lake Champlain">Lake Champlain</a> and demilitarized the border between the U.S. and <a href="/wiki/British_North_America" title="British North America">British North America</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_1818" title="Treaty of 1818">Treaty of 1818</a>, also with Great Britain, was concluded October 20, 1818, and fixed the present <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_border" title="Canada–United States border">Canada–United States border</a> from <a href="/wiki/Minnesota" title="Minnesota">Minnesota</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Rocky_Mountains" title="Rocky Mountains">Rocky Mountains</a> at the <a href="/wiki/49th_parallel_north" title="49th parallel north">49th parallel</a>. The accords also established a joint U.S.–British occupation of <a href="/wiki/Oregon_Country" title="Oregon Country">Oregon Country</a> for the next ten years.<sup id="cite_ref-JMforeign_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JMforeign-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Though they did not solve every outstanding issue between the U.S. and Britain, the treaties allowed for greater trade between the United States and the British Empire and helped avoid an expensive naval arms race in the Great Lakes.<sup id="cite_ref-sdmilestone_109-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sdmilestone-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Late in Monroe's second term, the U.S. concluded the <a href="/wiki/Russo-American_Treaty_of_1824" title="Russo-American Treaty of 1824">Russo-American Treaty of 1824</a> with the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russian Empire</a>, setting the southern limit of Russian sovereignty on the Pacific coast of <a href="/wiki/North_America" title="North America">North America</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Parallel_54%C2%B040%E2%80%B2_north" class="mw-redirect" title="Parallel 54°40′ north">54°40′ parallel</a> (the present southern tip of the <a href="/wiki/Alaska_Panhandle" class="mw-redirect" title="Alaska Panhandle">Alaska Panhandle</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Acquisition_of_Florida">Acquisition of Florida</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty" title="Adams–Onís Treaty">Adams–Onís Treaty</a> and <a href="/wiki/Seminole_Wars" title="Seminole Wars">Seminole Wars</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Adams_onis_map.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Adams_onis_map.png/220px-Adams_onis_map.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Adams_onis_map.png/330px-Adams_onis_map.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/96/Adams_onis_map.png/440px-Adams_onis_map.png 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="654" /></a><figcaption>Map showing the results of the <a href="/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty" title="Adams–Onís Treaty">Adams–Onís Treaty</a> of 1819</figcaption></figure>
<p>Spain had long rejected repeated American efforts to purchase <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Florida" title="Spanish Florida">Florida</a>. But by 1818, Spain was facing a troubling colonial situation in which the cession of Florida made sense. Spain had been exhausted by the <a href="/wiki/Peninsular_War" title="Peninsular War">Peninsular War</a> in Europe and needed to rebuild its credibility and presence in its colonies. Revolutionaries in <a href="/wiki/Central_America" title="Central America">Central America</a> and <a href="/wiki/South_America" title="South America">South America</a> were beginning to demand independence. Spain was unwilling to invest further in Florida, encroached on by American settlers, and it worried about the border between <a href="/wiki/New_Spain" title="New Spain">New Spain</a> and the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a>. With only a minor military presence in Florida, Spain was not able to restrain the <a href="/wiki/Seminole" title="Seminole">Seminole</a> warriors who routinely crossed the border and raided American villages and farms, as well as protected southern slave refugees from slave owners and traders of the southern United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWeeks1992118_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeeks1992118-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> The Seminole people were also providing sanctuary for runaway slaves, those of which the United States wanted back.<sup id="cite_ref-seminolenationmuseum.org_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-seminolenationmuseum.org-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In response to Seminole attacks and their provision of aid to escaped slaves, Monroe ordered a military expedition to cross into Spanish Florida and attack the Seminoles. In this expedition, led by <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson" title="Andrew Jackson">Andrew Jackson</a>, the US Army displaced numerous Seminole people from their houses along with burning their towns. Jackson also seized the Spanish territorial capital of <a href="/wiki/Pensacola,_Florida" title="Pensacola, Florida">Pensacola</a>. With the capture of Pensacola, Jackson established de facto American control of the entire territory. While Monroe supported Jackson's actions, many in Congress harshly criticized what they saw as an undeclared war. With the support of Secretary of State Adams, Monroe defended Jackson against domestic and international criticism, and the United States began negotiations with Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-seminolenationmuseum.org_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-seminolenationmuseum.org-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Spain faced revolt in all of its American colonies and could neither govern nor defend Florida. On February 22, 1819, Spain and the United States signed the <a href="/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty" title="Adams–Onís Treaty">Adams–Onís Treaty</a>, which ceded <a href="/wiki/The_Floridas" title="The Floridas">the Floridas</a> in return for the assumption by the United States of claims of American citizens against Spain to an amount not exceeding $5,000,000 (~$128&#160;million in 2021). The treaty also contained a definition of the boundary between Spanish and American possessions on the North American continent. Beginning at the mouth of the <a href="/wiki/Sabine_River_(Texas-Louisiana)" class="mw-redirect" title="Sabine River (Texas-Louisiana)">Sabine River</a> the line ran along that river to the <a href="/wiki/32nd_parallel_north" title="32nd parallel north">32nd parallel</a>, then due north to the <a href="/wiki/Red_River_of_the_South" title="Red River of the South">Red River</a>, which it followed to the <a href="/wiki/100th_meridian_west" title="100th meridian west">100th meridian</a>, due north to the <a href="/wiki/Arkansas_River" title="Arkansas River">Arkansas River</a>, and along that river to its <a href="/wiki/Headwaters" class="mw-redirect" title="Headwaters">source</a>, then north to the <a href="/wiki/42nd_parallel_north" title="42nd parallel north">42nd parallel</a>, which it followed to the <a href="/wiki/Pacific_Ocean" title="Pacific Ocean">Pacific Ocean</a>. As the United States renounced all claims to the west and south of this boundary (<a href="/wiki/Spanish_Texas" title="Spanish Texas">Texas, New Mexico, Arizona, California, Colorado, Utah, Nevada</a>), so Spain surrendered any title she had to the Northwest (<a href="/wiki/Oregon_Country" title="Oregon Country">Oregon Country</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJohnson1915262–264_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson1915262–264-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Monroe_Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:GSJamesMonroe.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/GSJamesMonroe.jpg/220px-GSJamesMonroe.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="275" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/GSJamesMonroe.jpg/330px-GSJamesMonroe.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/GSJamesMonroe.jpg/440px-GSJamesMonroe.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="2500" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of President Monroe by <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Stuart" title="Gilbert Stuart">Gilbert Stuart</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1820–1822</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Monroe was deeply sympathetic to the <a href="/wiki/Latin_American_wars_of_independence" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin American wars of independence">Latin American revolutionary movements</a> against Spain. He was determined that the United States should never repeat the policies of the Washington administration during the French Revolution, when the nation had failed to demonstrate its sympathy for the aspirations of peoples seeking to establish republican governments. He did not envisage military involvement in Latin American affairs, but only the provision of moral support, as he believed that a direct American intervention would provoke other European powers into assisting Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971476–492_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971476–492-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Monroe initially refused to recognize the Latin American governments due to ongoing negotiations with Spain over Florida.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECunningham1996105–106_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECunningham1996105–106-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 1822, Monroe officially recognized the countries of <a href="/wiki/Argentina" title="Argentina">Argentina</a>, <a href="/wiki/Peru" title="Peru">Peru</a>, <a href="/wiki/Colombia" title="Colombia">Colombia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chile" title="Chile">Chile</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Mexico" title="Mexico">Mexico</a>, all of which had won <a href="/wiki/Latin_American_wars_of_independence" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin American wars of independence">independence</a> from Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-JMforeign_111-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JMforeign-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Secretary of State Adams, under Monroe's supervision, wrote the instructions for the ministers to these new countries. They declared that the policy of the United States was to uphold republican institutions and to seek treaties of commerce on a most-favored-nation basis. The United States would support inter-American congresses dedicated to the development of economic and political institutions fundamentally differing from those prevailing in Europe. Monroe took pride as the United States was the first nation to extend recognition and to set an example to the rest of the world for its support of the "cause of liberty and humanity".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971476–492_118-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971476–492-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For their part, the British also had a strong interest in ensuring the demise of Spanish colonialism, with all the trade restrictions <a href="/wiki/Mercantilism" title="Mercantilism">mercantilism</a> imposed. In October 1823, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Rush" title="Richard Rush">Richard Rush</a>, the American minister in London, advised that Foreign Secretary <a href="/wiki/George_Canning" title="George Canning">George Canning</a> was proposing that the U.S. and Britain issue a joint declaration to deter any other power from intervening in Central and South America. Adams vigorously opposed cooperation with Great Britain, contending that a statement of bilateral nature could limit United States expansion in the future. He also argued that the British were not committed to recognizing the Latin American republics and must have had imperial motivations themselves.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Two months later, the bilateral statement proposed by the British became a unilateral declaration by the United States. While Monroe thought that Spain was unlikely to re-establish its colonial empire on its own, he feared that France or the <a href="/wiki/Holy_Alliance" title="Holy Alliance">Holy Alliance</a> might seek to establish control over the former Spanish possessions.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> On December 2, 1823, in his annual message to Congress, Monroe articulated what became known as the Monroe Doctrine. He first reiterated the traditional U.S. policy of neutrality with regard to European wars and conflicts. He then declared that the United States would not accept the recolonization of any country by its former European master, though he also avowed non-interference with existing European colonies in the Americas.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Finally, he stated that European countries should no longer consider the Western Hemisphere open to new colonization, a jab aimed primarily at Russia, which was attempting to expand its colony on the northern Pacific Coast.<sup id="cite_ref-JMforeign_111-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JMforeign-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971476–492_118-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971476–492-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1820">Election of 1820</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1820_United_States_presidential_election" title="1820 United States presidential election">1820 United States presidential election</a></div>
<p>The collapse of the Federalists left Monroe with no organized opposition at the end of his first term, and he ran for reelection unopposed,<sup id="cite_ref-miller.monroe.elections_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-miller.monroe.elections-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> the only president other than <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">Washington</a> to do so. A single elector from New Hampshire, <a href="/wiki/William_Plumer" title="William Plumer">William Plumer</a>, cast a vote for <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a>, preventing a unanimous vote in the Electoral College.<sup id="cite_ref-miller.monroe.elections_123-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-miller.monroe.elections-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> He did so because he thought Monroe was incompetent. Later in the century, the story arose that he had cast his dissenting vote so that only George Washington would have the honor of unanimous election. Plumer never mentioned Washington in his speech explaining his vote to the other New Hampshire electors.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="States_admitted_to_the_Union">States admitted to the Union</span></h3>
<p>Five new states were <a href="/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union" title="Admission to the Union">admitted to the Union</a> while Monroe was in office:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Mississippi" title="Mississippi">Mississippi</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;December 10, 1817<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Illinois" title="Illinois">Illinois</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;December 3, 1818<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Alabama" title="Alabama">Alabama</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;December 14, 1819<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Maine" title="Maine">Maine</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;March 15, 1820<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Missouri" title="Missouri">Missouri</a>&#160;&#8211;&#32;August 10, 1821<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span id="Post-presidency_.281825.E2.80.931831.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-presidency_(1825–1831)">Post-presidency (1825–1831)</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:James_Monroe_marker_at_Univ._of_VA_IMG_4248.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/James_Monroe_marker_at_Univ._of_VA_IMG_4248.JPG/220px-James_Monroe_marker_at_Univ._of_VA_IMG_4248.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/James_Monroe_marker_at_Univ._of_VA_IMG_4248.JPG/330px-James_Monroe_marker_at_Univ._of_VA_IMG_4248.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/James_Monroe_marker_at_Univ._of_VA_IMG_4248.JPG/440px-James_Monroe_marker_at_Univ._of_VA_IMG_4248.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4320" data-file-height="3240" /></a><figcaption>Monroe once owned a farm at the location of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Virginia" title="University of Virginia">University of Virginia</a> in <a href="/wiki/Charlottesville,_Virginia" title="Charlottesville, Virginia">Charlottesville</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chester_Harding_-_James_Monroe_-_NPG.2005.44_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Chester_Harding_-_James_Monroe_-_NPG.2005.44_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg/220px-Chester_Harding_-_James_Monroe_-_NPG.2005.44_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="266" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Chester_Harding_-_James_Monroe_-_NPG.2005.44_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg/330px-Chester_Harding_-_James_Monroe_-_NPG.2005.44_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Chester_Harding_-_James_Monroe_-_NPG.2005.44_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg/440px-Chester_Harding_-_James_Monroe_-_NPG.2005.44_-_National_Portrait_Gallery.jpg 2x" data-file-width="620" data-file-height="750" /></a><figcaption>Monroe in a 1829 portrait by <a href="/wiki/Chester_Harding_(painter)" title="Chester Harding (painter)">Chester Harding</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>When his presidency ended on March 4, 1825, James Monroe resided at <a href="/wiki/Brown_College_at_Monroe_Hill" title="Brown College at Monroe Hill">Monroe Hill</a>, what is now included in the grounds of the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Virginia" title="University of Virginia">University of Virginia</a>. He served on the university's Board of Visitors under Jefferson and under the second rector <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">James Madison</a>, both former presidents, almost until his death. He and his wife lived at <a href="/wiki/Oak_Hill_(James_Monroe_house)" title="Oak Hill (James Monroe house)">Oak Hill</a> in <a href="/wiki/Aldie,_Virginia" title="Aldie, Virginia">Aldie, Virginia</a>, until Elizabeth's death at age 62 on September 23, 1830. In August 1825, the Monroes had received the <a href="/wiki/Marquis_de_Lafayette" class="mw-redirect" title="Marquis de Lafayette">Marquis de Lafayette</a> and President <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a> as guests there.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Monroe incurred many unliquidated debts during his years of public life. He sold off his <a href="/wiki/Highland_(James_Monroe_house)" title="Highland (James Monroe house)">Highland Plantation</a>. It is now owned by his <i>alma mater</i>, the <a href="/wiki/College_of_William_and_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="College of William and Mary">College of William and Mary</a>, which has opened it to the public as a historic site. Throughout his life, he was financially insolvent, which was exacerbated by his wife's poor health.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Monroe was elected as a delegate to the <a href="/wiki/Virginia_Constitutional_Convention_of_1829%E2%80%931830" title="Virginia Constitutional Convention of 1829–1830">Virginia Constitutional Convention of 1829–1830</a>. He was one of four delegates elected from the senatorial district made up of his home district of Loudoun and Fairfax County.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> In October 1829, he was elected by the convention to serve as the presiding officer, until his failing health required him to withdraw on December 8, after which <a href="/wiki/Philip_P._Barbour" title="Philip P. Barbour">Philip P. Barbour</a> of Orange County was elected presiding officer.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Monroe_Tomb_02.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Monroe_Tomb_02.jpg/170px-Monroe_Tomb_02.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="262" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Monroe_Tomb_02.jpg/255px-Monroe_Tomb_02.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Monroe_Tomb_02.jpg/340px-Monroe_Tomb_02.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2368" data-file-height="3656" /></a><figcaption>Monroe's grave at <a href="/wiki/Hollywood_Cemetery_(Richmond,_Virginia)" title="Hollywood Cemetery (Richmond, Virginia)">Hollywood Cemetery</a> in <a href="/wiki/Richmond,_Virginia" title="Richmond, Virginia">Richmond, Virginia</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Upon Elizabeth's death in 1830, Monroe moved to 63 Prince Street at Lafayette Place<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a> to live with his daughter Maria Hester Monroe Gouverneur, who had married <a href="/wiki/Samuel_L._Gouverneur" title="Samuel L. Gouverneur">Samuel L. Gouverneur</a>. Monroe's health began to slowly fail by the end of the 1820s.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> On July 4, 1831, Monroe died at age 73 from <a href="/wiki/Heart_failure" title="Heart failure">heart failure</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a>, thus becoming the third president to have died on <a href="/wiki/Independence_Day_(United_States)" title="Independence Day (United States)">Independence Day</a>. His death came 55 years after the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Declaration_of_Independence" title="United States Declaration of Independence">United States Declaration of Independence</a> was proclaimed and five years after the deaths of John Adams and Thomas Jefferson. Monroe was originally buried in New York at the Gouverneur family's vault in the <a href="/wiki/New_York_City_Marble_Cemetery" title="New York City Marble Cemetery">New York City Marble Cemetery</a>. 27 years later, in 1858, his body was re-interred at the President's Circle in <a href="/wiki/Hollywood_Cemetery_(Richmond,_Virginia)" title="Hollywood Cemetery (Richmond, Virginia)">Hollywood Cemetery</a> in <a href="/wiki/Richmond,_Virginia" title="Richmond, Virginia">Richmond, Virginia</a>. The <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe_Tomb" title="James Monroe Tomb">James Monroe Tomb</a> is a U.S. <a href="/wiki/National_Historic_Landmark" title="National Historic Landmark">National Historic Landmark</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Religious_beliefs">Religious beliefs</span></h2>
<p>"When it comes to Monroe's thoughts on religion," historian Bliss Isely notes, "less is known than that of any other President." No letters survive in which he discussed his religious beliefs. Nor did his friends, family or associates comment on his beliefs. Letters that do survive, such as ones written after the death of his son, contain no discussion of religion.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Monroe was raised in a family that belonged to the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> when it was the state church in Virginia before the Revolution. As an adult, he attended <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_Church_in_the_United_States_of_America" class="mw-redirect" title="Episcopal Church in the United States of America">Episcopal</a> churches. Some historians see "deistic tendencies" in his few references to an impersonal God.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Unlike Jefferson, Monroe was rarely attacked as an atheist or infidel. In 1832 James Renwick Willson, a <a href="/wiki/Reformed_Presbyterian_Church_of_North_America" title="Reformed Presbyterian Church of North America">Reformed Presbyterian</a> minister in Albany, New York, criticized Monroe for having "lived and died like a second-rate Athenian philosopher".<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Slavery">Slavery</span></h2>
<p>Monroe owned dozens of <a href="/wiki/Slavery" title="Slavery">slaves</a>. He took several slaves with him to Washington to serve at the White House from 1817 to 1825. This was typical of other slaveholding presidents.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As president of Virginia's constitutional convention in the fall of 1829, Monroe reiterated his belief that slavery was a blight which, even as a British colony, Virginia had attempted to eradicate. "What was the origin of our slave population?" he rhetorically asked. "The evil commenced when we were in our Colonial state, but acts were passed by our Colonial Legislature, prohibiting the importation, of more slaves, into the Colony. These were rejected by the Crown." To the dismay of states' rights proponents, he was willing to accept the federal government's financial assistance to emancipate and transport freed slaves to other countries. At the convention, Monroe made his final public statement on slavery, proposing that Virginia <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">emancipate</a> and deport its bondsmen with "the aid of the Union".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971563–566_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971563–566-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Monroe was Governor of Virginia in 1800, hundreds of slaves from Virginia planned to kidnap him, take <a href="/wiki/Richmond,_Virginia" title="Richmond, Virginia">Richmond</a>, and negotiate for their freedom. <a href="/wiki/Gabriel%27s_Rebellion" title="Gabriel&#39;s Rebellion">Gabriel's slave conspiracy</a> was discovered.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Monroe called out the militia; the slave patrols soon captured some slaves accused of involvement. Sidbury says some trials had a few measures to prevent abuses, such as an appointed attorney, but they were "hardly 'fair'". <a href="/wiki/Slave_codes" title="Slave codes">Slave codes</a> prevented slaves from being treated like whites, and they were given quick trials without a jury.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Monroe influenced the Executive Council to pardon and sell some slaves instead of hanging them.<sup id="cite_ref-145" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Historians say the Virginia courts executed between 26 and 35 slaves. None of the executed slaves had killed any whites because the uprising had been foiled before it began.<sup id="cite_ref-aptheker_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-aptheker-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> An additional 50 slaves charged for their role in the planned rebellion would be spared, as a result of pardons, acquittals, and commutations. One reason for this was influence of a letter Monroe received from <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a> urging mercy, telling him "The other states &amp; the world at large will for ever condemn us if we indulge a principle of revenge, or go one step beyond absolute necessity. They cannot lose sight of the rights of the two parties, &amp; the object of the unsuccessful one." Only seven of the executions carried out against the rebels occurred after Monroe received Jefferson's letter.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Monroe was active in the <a href="/wiki/American_Colonization_Society" title="American Colonization Society">American Colonization Society</a>, which supported the establishment of colonies outside of the United States for free African Americans. The society helped send several thousand freed slaves to the new colony of <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a> in Africa from 1820 to 1840. Slave owners like Monroe and Andrew Jackson wanted to prevent free blacks from encouraging slaves in the South to rebel. Liberia's capital, <a href="/wiki/Monrovia" title="Monrovia">Monrovia</a>, was named after President Monroe.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971522–523_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmmon1971522–523-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JamesMonroeStatue.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/JamesMonroeStatue.jpg/240px-JamesMonroeStatue.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="320" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/JamesMonroeStatue.jpg/360px-JamesMonroeStatue.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/JamesMonroeStatue.jpg/480px-JamesMonroeStatue.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1056" data-file-height="1408" /></a><figcaption>Statue of Monroe at <a href="/wiki/Highland_(James_Monroe_house)" title="Highland (James Monroe house)">Highland</a>, his house near <a href="/wiki/Charlottesville,_Virginia" title="Charlottesville, Virginia">Charlottesville, Virginia</a></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Historical_reputation">Historical reputation</span></h3>
<p>Polls of historians and political scientists tend to <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">rank</a> Monroe as an above average president.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Monroe presided over a period in which the United States began to turn away from European affairs and towards domestic issues. His presidency saw the United States settle many of its longstanding boundary issues through an accommodation with Britain and the acquisition of Florida. Monroe also helped resolve sectional tensions through his support of the Missouri Compromise and by seeking support from all regions of the country.<sup id="cite_ref-millerlegacy_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-millerlegacy-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Political scientist Fred Greenstein argues that Monroe was a more effective executive than some of his better-known predecessors, including Madison and John Adams.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreenstein2009275_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreenstein2009275-152">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memorials">Memorials</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_memorials_to_James_Monroe" title="List of memorials to James Monroe">List of memorials to James Monroe</a></div>
<p>The capital of <a href="/wiki/Liberia" title="Liberia">Liberia</a> is named <a href="/wiki/Monrovia" title="Monrovia">Monrovia</a> after Monroe; it is the only <a href="/wiki/List_of_national_capitals" title="List of national capitals">national capital</a> other than <a href="/wiki/Washington,_D.C." title="Washington, D.C.">Washington, D.C.</a>, named after a U.S. president. Monroe is the namesake of seventeen <a href="/wiki/Monroe_County_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Monroe County (disambiguation)">Monroe counties</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-153" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Monroe,_Maine" title="Monroe, Maine">Monroe, Maine</a>, <a href="/wiki/Monroe,_Michigan" title="Monroe, Michigan">Monroe, Michigan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Monroe,_Georgia" title="Monroe, Georgia">Monroe, Georgia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Monroe,_Connecticut" title="Monroe, Connecticut">Monroe, Connecticut</a>, both <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Township,_New_Jersey_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Monroe Township, New Jersey (disambiguation)">Monroe Townships in New Jersey</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Fort_Monroe" title="Fort Monroe">Fort Monroe</a> are all named for him. Monroe has been depicted on U.S. currency and stamps, including a 1954 <a href="/wiki/United_States_Postal_Service" title="United States Postal Service">United States Postal Service</a> 5¢ <a href="/wiki/Liberty_Issue" title="Liberty Issue">Liberty Issue</a> <a href="/wiki/Postage_stamp" title="Postage stamp">postage stamp</a>.
</p><p>Monroe was the last U.S. president to wear a <a href="/wiki/Wig#19th_and_20th_centuries" title="Wig">powdered wig tied in a queue</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Tricorne" title="Tricorne">tricorne hat</a> and <a href="/wiki/Culottes" title="Culottes">knee-breeches</a> according to the <a href="/wiki/1775%E2%80%931795_in_Western_fashion#Men&#39;s_fashion" title="1775–1795 in Western fashion">style of the late 18th century</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> That earned him <a href="/wiki/List_of_nicknames_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="List of nicknames of presidents of the United States">the nickname</a> "The Last Cocked Hat". He was also the last president who was not photographed.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-columns references-column-width reflist-lower-alpha" style="column-width: 35em;">
<ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-130"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-130">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Maine is one of 3 states that were set off from already existing states (Kentucky and West Virginia are the others). The <a href="/wiki/Massachusetts_General_Court" title="Massachusetts General Court">Massachusetts General Court</a> passed enabling legislation on June 19, 1819, separating the "<a href="/wiki/District_of_Maine" title="District of Maine">District of Maine</a>" from the rest of the State (an action approved by the voters in Maine on July 19, 1819, by 17,001 to 7,132); then, on February 25, 1820, passed a follow-up measure officially accepting the fact of Maine's imminent statehood.<sup id="cite_ref-GP_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-GP-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
</ol></div>
